<a id='question-028'></a>

### Question 28: Autosomal Monosomy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Autosomal monosomy (loss of a complete autosome) is incompatible with life due to haploinsufficiency of essential genes, whereas sex monosomy (e.g., Turner syndrome, 45,X) is viable because of X-chromosome inactivation. (常染色体单倍体由于必需基因的半合子不足而与生命不相容，而性染色体单倍体（如X染色体）由于X失活而可行存。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman presents to a genetics clinic after experiencing two consecutive spontaneous miscarriages in the first trimester. She and her husband have undergone extensive infertility workup, including hormonal assays, uterine evaluation, and semen analysis, all of which are normal. Karyotyping of the couple reveals that the husband has a normal 46,XY karyotype. The woman's karyotype is 46,XX. However, analysis of products of conception (POC) from both miscarriages reveals a mosaic karyotype of 46,XX,45,X[10]/46,XX[90] in the first miscarriage and 46,XX,45,X[5]/46,XX[95] in the second miscarriage. The woman is otherwise healthy, with normal physical examination findings and no reported history of congenital anomalies. She has regular menstrual cycles and normal ovarian function. Her mother had three children, all female, and her father had four children, two male and two female. There is no known family history of genetic disorders or recurrent miscarriages. Given the recurrent miscarriages and the mosaic karyotypes found in the POC, what is the most likely underlying genetic mechanism responsible for these pregnancy losses?

(A) Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte leading to monosomy 21, resulting in a lethal autosomal trisomy 21 in the zygote.
(B) Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte leading to monosomy 13, resulting in a lethal autosomal trisomy 13 in the zygote.
(C) Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte leading to monosomy X, resulting in a viable mosaicism of 46,XX/45,X in the fetus.
(D) Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte leading to monosomy 18, resulting in a lethal autosomal trisomy 18 in the zygote.
(E) Meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte leading to monosomy 22, resulting in a lethal autosomal trisomy 22 in the zygote.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent Miscarriages:** Suggests a genetic or chromosomal abnormality in the fetus or parent.
- **Normal Parental Karyotypes (46,XY and 46,XX):** Rules out balanced translocations or inversions in the parents as the primary cause, although parental mosaicism is possible but less likely given the context.
- **Mosaic Karyotypes in POC (46,XX,45,X):** Indicates that some conceptuses have a missing X chromosome (monosomy X) alongside a normal XX complement. The presence of 45,X cells is the critical finding.
- **Viability of the Mother (46,XX):** Demonstrates that monosomy X (45,X) is compatible with life, albeit associated with Turner syndrome.
- **Lethality of Autosomal Monosomy:** The question implicitly tests the knowledge that monosomy of any autosome is lethal. The POC results show mosaicism involving 45,X, not 45,A (where A is an autosome).
- **Mechanism:** The mosaicism (46,XX/45,X) arises from meiotic nondisjunction of the X chromosomes in the oocyte. This results in an egg with either no X chromosome (23,0) or two X chromosomes (23,XX). Fertilization by a normal sperm (23,Y) leads to a zygote with either 45,X (monosomy X) or 47,XXY (Klinefelter syndrome). Fertilization by a normal sperm (23,X) leads to 46,XX or 48,XXX. The 45,X zygote is usually lethal early in development, but sometimes survives as a mosaic (e.g., 46,XX/45,X) due to mitotic errors or loss of the extra X chromosome in a 48,XXY or 48,XXX zygote. The 47,XXY zygote is also often lethal, but can sometimes survive as a mosaic (e.g., 46,XY/47,XXY). The 46,XX and 48,XXX zygotes are typically viable. The recurrent miscarriages are most likely due to the loss of conceptuses with a significant proportion of 45,X cells, which often fail to develop properly.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Meiosis I Nondisjunction:** During oogenesis, homologous chromosomes (XX) fail to separate properly in Meiosis I.
- **Meiosis II Nondisjunction:** Alternatively, sister chromatids fail to separate properly in Meiosis II.
- **Resulting Gametes:** Both nondisjunction events lead to gametes with an abnormal number of sex chromosomes: nullisomic (0X), monosomic (1X), disomic (2X).
- **Fertilization:**
    - Nullisomic oocyte (0X) + Normal sperm (X) -> 45,X zygote (usually lethal)
    - Monosomic oocyte (1X) + Normal sperm (X) -> 46,XX zygote (viable)
    - Disomic oocyte (2X) + Normal sperm (X) -> 47,XXX zygote (viable)
    - Nullisomic oocyte (0X) + Normal sperm (Y) -> 45,X zygote (usually lethal)
    - Monosomic oocyte (1X) + Normal sperm (Y) -> 46,XY zygote (viable)
    - Disomic oocyte (2X) + Normal sperm (Y) -> 47,XXY zygote (Klinefelter, often lethal or mosaic)
- **Mosaicism:** If a 47,XXX or 47,XXY zygote undergoes mitotic errors (e.g., loss of a chromosome), it can result in a mosaic like 46,XX/45,X or 46,XY/47,XXY.
- **Autosomal Monosomy:** Monosomy of any autosome (e.g., 45,A) is incompatible with life because these autosomes contain numerous essential genes. Having only one copy of these genes leads to haploinsufficiency, resulting in severe developmental defects and early embryonic lethality.
- **Sex Chromosome Monosomy:** Monosomy X (45,X) is compatible with life, although it causes Turner syndrome. This is thought to be because the X chromosome contains fewer essential genes compared to autosomes, and the dosage of X-linked genes can be partially compensated by the single X chromosome and inactivation of the single X chromosome.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Meiotic nondisjunction leading to monosomy 21 would result in a 45,21 zygote. Fertilization by a normal sperm (23,X or 23,Y) would lead to a 46,XX,21 or 46,XY,21 zygote (Down syndrome) or a 46,X,21 or 46,Y,21 zygote. Monosomy 21 itself (45,21) is lethal. Trisomy 21 (47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21) is viable. The vignette describes mosaicism involving 45,X, not 45,21. Autosomal monosomy 21 is lethal, but the POC show 45,X mosaicism, not 45,21.
- (B): Meiotic nondisjunction leading to monosomy 13 would result in a 45,13 zygote. Fertilization by a normal sperm would lead to 46,XX,13 or 46,XY,13. Autosomal monosomy 13 is lethal. Trisomy 13 (47,XX,+13 or 47,XY,+13) is viable but associated with severe defects (Patau syndrome). The vignette describes mosaicism involving 45,X, not 45,13.
- (D): Meiotic nondisjunction leading to monosomy 18 would result in a 45,18 zygote. Fertilization by a normal sperm would lead to 46,XX,18 or 46,XY,18. Autosomal monosomy 18 is lethal. Trisomy 18 (47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18) is viable but associated with severe defects (Edwards syndrome). The vignette describes mosaicism involving 45,X, not 45,18.
- (E): Meiotic nondisjunction leading to monosomy 22 would result in a 45,22 zygote. Fertilization by a normal sperm would lead to 46,XX,22 or 46,XY,22. Autosomal monosomy 22 is lethal. Trisomy 22 (47,XX,+22 or 47,XY,+22) is viable. The vignette describes mosaicism involving 45,X, not 45,22.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Autosomal monosomy is lethal due to haploinsufficiency of essential genes located on autosomes. Sex chromosome monosomy (45,X) is viable, likely due to fewer essential genes on the X chromosome and X-inactivation.
- Differential diagnosis for recurrent miscarriages includes parental chromosomal abnormalities (translocations, inversions), uterine abnormalities, endocrine disorders, autoimmune disorders, and fetal chromosomal abnormalities (aneuploidy, structural rearrangements). The mosaic karyotype in the POC points specifically to a meiotic error in the mother leading to monosomy X.

<a id='question-029'></a>

### Question 029: Meiotic Nondisjunction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Meiotic nondisjunction is the failure of homologous chromosomes (Meiosis I) or sister chromatids (Meiosis II) to separate properly during meiosis, leading to aneuploidy (abnormal chromosome number) in gametes and subsequent offspring. This question tests the understanding of the molecular basis and consequences of meiotic nondisjunction, specifically focusing on the timing and resulting chromosomal abnormalities. (Meiosis nondisjunction 是减数分裂过程中同源染色体或姐妹染色单体分离不正常，导致配子和后代染色体数目异常 (aneuploidy) 的主要原因。 本题考察对减数分裂 nondisjunction 的分子基础和后果的理解，特别是 nondisjunction 的时机和由此产生的染色体异常。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, gravida 2, para 1, presents for prenatal counseling. Her first pregnancy resulted in the birth of a healthy son. However, her current pregnancy is at high risk based on advanced maternal age (38 years old at conception) and results from non-invasive prenatal testing (NIPT) showing a high probability for fetal aneuploidy. Chorionic villus sampling (CVS) is performed, and karyotyping of fetal cells reveals a 47,XY,+21 karyotype. The parents undergo karyotyping, which shows a normal 46,XX karyotype for the mother and a normal 46,XY karyotype for the father. The mother reports no history of previous miscarriages or stillbirths. The couple is concerned about the recurrence risk in future pregnancies.

(A) The most likely cause of the fetal aneuploidy is nondisjunction during spermatogenesis in the father, resulting in a gamete with an extra chromosome 21.
(B) The most likely cause of the fetal aneuploidy is nondisjunction during oogenesis in the mother, occurring during Meiosis I, resulting in a gamete with an extra chromosome 21.
(C) The most likely cause of the fetal aneuploidy is nondisjunction during oogenesis in the mother, occurring during Meiosis II, resulting in a gamete with an extra chromosome 21.
(D) The most likely cause of the fetal aneuploidy is nondisjunction during spermatogenesis in the father, occurring during Meiosis II, resulting in a gamete with an extra chromosome 21.
(E) The most likely cause of the fetal aneuploidy is a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 21 in the mother, leading to an unbalanced gamete.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old pregnant woman (gravida 2, para 1), advanced maternal age (38 at conception). Advanced maternal age is a significant risk factor for meiotic nondisjunction in oogenesis.
*   **Prenatal Screening:** NIPT indicates high probability for fetal aneuploidy.
*   **Diagnostic Test:** CVS karyotype shows 47,XY,+21 (Trisomy 21, Down syndrome) in the fetus. The fetus is male (XY).
*   **Parental Karyotypes:** Both parents have normal karyotypes (46,XX mother, 46,XY father). This rules out balanced translocations or inversions in either parent as the primary cause of the *de novo* aneuploidy in the fetus.
*   **Recurrence Risk Concern:** The couple is worried about future pregnancies.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the *most likely* cause of the fetal aneuploidy.

The key clues are the advanced maternal age, the normal parental karyotypes, and the fetal karyotype of 47,XY,+21. Trisomy 21 is most commonly caused by nondisjunction. Since the parents are karyotypically normal, the nondisjunction event is *de novo* (new mutation/event). Advanced maternal age strongly correlates with increased risk of nondisjunction during oogenesis.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Meiosis Overview:** Meiosis is a two-stage cell division process that produces gametes (sperm and egg) with half the number of chromosomes as the parent cell.
    *   **Meiosis I:** Homologous chromosomes pair up (synapsis) and then separate. Failure of homologous chromosomes to separate is called nondisjunction in Meiosis I. This results in gametes with either n+1 chromosomes (due to an extra homologous chromosome) or n-1 chromosomes (due to a missing homologous chromosome).
    *   **Meiosis II:** Sister chromatids separate. Failure of sister chromatids to separate is called nondisjunction in Meiosis II. This results in gametes with either n+1 chromosomes (due to an extra chromatid) or n-1 chromosomes (due to a missing chromatid).
*   **Nondisjunction Timing and Outcome:**
    *   **Meiosis I Nondisjunction:** If homologous chromosomes fail to separate in Meiosis I, both daughter cells entering Meiosis II will have an abnormal number of chromosomes. If these cells proceed through Meiosis II without further errors, the result will be two gametes with n+1 chromosomes and two gametes with n-1 chromosomes.
    *   **Meiosis II Nondisjunction:** If sister chromatids fail to separate in Meiosis II, the result will be one gamete with n+1 chromosomes, one gamete with n-1 chromosomes, and two normal gametes (n).
*   **Trisomy 21 (Down Syndrome):** The fetus has 47,XY,+21. This means there is an extra copy of chromosome 21. This can arise from:
    *   A normal egg (n=23) fertilized by a gamete with +21 (n+1=24).
    *   A gamete with +21 (n+1=24) fertilized by a normal sperm (n=23).
*   **Maternal Age and Nondisjunction:** The risk of nondisjunction, particularly for chromosome 21, increases significantly with maternal age. This is primarily attributed to prolonged arrest of oocytes in prophase I of Meiosis I during fetal development. This prolonged arrest increases the chance of errors in homologous chromosome pairing and separation. While paternal age also increases the risk of *de novo* mutations (e.g., point mutations) associated with conditions like achondroplasia, the risk of aneuploidy (like Trisomy 21) is much more strongly associated with maternal age and nondisjunction during oogenesis.
*   **Likely Scenario:** Given the advanced maternal age (38) and normal parental karyotypes, the most probable cause is nondisjunction during oogenesis in the mother. Nondisjunction in Meiosis I is more common than in Meiosis II and accounts for the majority (~90%) of trisomies. If nondisjunction occurs in Meiosis I, the mother produces an egg with 24 chromosomes (including two copies of chromosome 21). Fertilization by a normal sperm (23 chromosomes) results in a zygote with 47 chromosomes, including trisomy 21 (47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21, depending on the sperm).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While paternal nondisjunction can cause Trisomy 21, the risk is much lower than maternal nondisjunction, especially considering the mother's advanced age (38). Paternal age is more strongly linked to *de novo* point mutations, not aneuploidy. The question asks for the *most likely* cause. (错误。虽然父方 nondisjunction 可以导致 Trisomy 21，但风险远低于母方 nondisjunction，特别是考虑到母亲的年龄 (38 岁)。父方年龄与 *de novo* 点突变更相关，而非染色体数目异常。问题要求最可能的原因。)
- (C): **Incorrect.** Nondisjunction in Meiosis II during oogenesis would produce an egg with 23 chromosomes, but one chromosome would be duplicated (e.g., two copies of chromosome 21). This egg (n+1=24) fertilized by a normal sperm (n=23) would result in a zygote with 47 chromosomes, including trisomy 21. However, Meiosis I nondisjunction is significantly more common (~90% of trisomy 21 cases) than Meiosis II nondisjunction (~10%). Therefore, Meiosis I is the *most likely* event. (错误。Meiosis II 期间的母方 nondisjunction 会产生一个带有重复染色体的卵子 (n+1=24)。这个卵子与精子 (n=23) 受精会产生一个带有 Trisomy 21 的合子 (47 染色体)。然而，Meiosis I nondisjunction 比 Meiosis II nondisjunction 更常见 (~90% vs ~10%)。因此，Meiosis I 是*最可能*的事件。)
- (D): **Incorrect.** Similar to (A), paternal nondisjunction is less likely than maternal nondisjunction, especially given the mother's age. Also, the specific timing (Meiosis II) is less common than Meiosis I nondisjunction. (错误。类似于 (A)，父方 nondisjunction 的可能性低于母方 nondisjunction，特别是考虑到母亲的年龄。此外，Meiosis II 的 timing 也比 Meiosis I 更罕见。)
- (E): **Incorrect.** A Robertsonian translocation involves the fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (like 21 and 14). While this *can* lead to Trisomy 21 (e.g., if the parent carries a balanced translocation t(14;21) and passes on the translocated chromosome plus a normal chromosome 21), the parental karyotypes were reported as normal (46,XX and 46,XY). This rules out a balanced translocation in either parent as the cause of this *de novo* aneuploidy. (错误。Robertsonian translocation 涉及两个着丝粒染色体的融合 (例如 21 号和 14 号染色体)。这*可能*导致 Trisomy 21 (例如，如果父母携带平衡易位 t(14;21) 并传递易位染色体和一个正常的 21 号染色体)，但父母的核型报告为正常 (46,XX 和 46,XY)。这排除了父母一方携带平衡易位作为导致这种 *de novo* 染色体数目异常的原因。)

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Meiotic nondisjunction is the primary cause of aneuploidy, with maternal age being the most significant risk factor, particularly for Trisomy 21. Nondisjunction in Meiosis I is more common than in Meiosis II. Understand the difference in outcomes based on the timing of the nondisjunction event. Key differentials include balanced chromosomal rearrangements (translocations, inversions) and *de novo* point mutations, but these are less likely given the normal parental karyotypes and the specific finding of trisomy. (Meiosis nondisjunction 是 aneuploidy 的主要原因，而母体年龄是风险最重要的因素，特别是对于 Trisomy 21。Meiosis I 的 nond

<a id='question-030'></a>

### Question 030: Nondisjunction in Meiosis I
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Nondisjunction during meiosis I leads to aneuploidy in gametes, specifically resulting in 50% of gametes having an extra chromosome (disomic) and 50% having a missing chromosome (nullisomic) for that homologous pair. This contrasts with meiosis II nondisjunction, which produces a mix of euploid, disomic, and nullisomic gametes. (减数第一次分裂的互补染色单体不等分离导致配子体细胞的染色体数目异常，50%的配子具有额外的染色体（二体），50%的配子缺少该染色体对（零体）。这与减数第二次分裂的不等分离不同，后者产生多种类型的配子：正常配子、二体配子和零体配子。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to a genetics clinic after experiencing three consecutive spontaneous miscarriages in the first trimester. She and her husband underwent comprehensive genetic testing. Karyotyping of the patient revealed a normal female karyotype (46,XX). However, analysis of a peripheral blood smear showed a mosaicism in her lymphocytes, with approximately 40% of cells exhibiting a 45,X karyotype (Turner syndrome) and 60% exhibiting a normal 46,XX karyotype. Her husband's karyotype was normal (46,XY). Fluorescence in situ hybridization (FISH) analysis using probes specific for the X chromosome was performed on metaphase spreads from the patient's lymphocytes. In the 45,X cells, FISH revealed only one X chromosome signal. In the 46,XX cells, FISH revealed two distinct X chromosome signals. The patient's mother had a history of multiple miscarriages but had a normal karyotype. The patient's father had a normal karyotype. Based on this information, what is the most likely mechanism responsible for the patient's mosaicism?

(A) Nondisjunction during meiosis II in the patient's mother, leading to a 45,X egg fertilized by a normal sperm.
(B) Nondisjunction during meiosis I in the patient's mother, leading to a 45,X egg and a 46,XX egg, both fertilized by a normal sperm.
(C) Nondisjunction during meiosis II in the patient's father, leading to a 45,X sperm and a 46,XY sperm, with the 45,X sperm fertilizing the patient's egg.
(D) Mitotic nondisjunction during early embryonic development after fertilization of a normal egg by a normal sperm.
(E) A translocation involving the X chromosome in the patient's mother, leading to an unbalanced gamete.

#### Correct Answer: (B)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female, recurrent spontaneous miscarriages (suggests potential chromosomal abnormality in gametes or embryo).
- **Patient Karyotype:** Mosaicism - 40% cells are 45,X (Turner syndrome) and 60% cells are 46,XX (normal female). This indicates that some cells lack an X chromosome, while others are normal.
- **Parental Karyotypes:** Both parents are karyotypically normal (46,XX for mother, 46,XY for father). This rules out a de novo mutation in the father or a pre-existing chromosomal abnormality in either parent that would directly explain the patient's mosaicism.
- **FISH Analysis:** Confirms the presence of one X chromosome in 45,X cells and two X chromosomes in 46,XX cells, consistent with monosomy X and disomy X, respectively.
- **Maternal History:** Mother had multiple miscarriages, suggesting a possible origin of the abnormality in her oogenesis.
- **Deduction:** The patient's mosaicism (45,X/46,XX) indicates that during her development, some cells received only one X chromosome, while others received two. Since the father contributes a Y chromosome (or an X in the case of sperm fertilizing a 45,X egg), the origin of the missing X chromosome must be maternal. The presence of both 45,X and 46,XX cell lines in the patient suggests that the mother produced two types of eggs: one lacking an X chromosome (45,X) and one with two X chromosomes (46,XX). The 45,X egg, when fertilized by a normal sperm (23,X), results in a 45,X zygote. The 46,XX egg, when fertilized by a normal sperm (23,X), results in a 46,XX zygote. This pattern is characteristic of nondisjunction during meiosis I in the mother.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Meiosis I vs. Meiosis II Nondisjunction:**
    - **Meiosis I:** Homologous chromosomes (in this case, the two X chromosomes) fail to separate during anaphase I. This results in two daughter cells, one receiving both X chromosomes (n+1) and the other receiving none (n-1). Upon fertilization by a normal sperm (23,X), this leads to two types of zygotes: one with three X chromosomes (47,XXX) and one with only one X chromosome (45,X). If the mother produces both types of eggs (n+1 and n-1) and they are fertilized by normal sperm, the resulting offspring will be mosaic for 45,X and 46,XX (if the 47,XXX zygote loses an X chromosome during mitosis, or if a 46,XX zygote arises from a different egg). The key feature of Meiosis I nondisjunction is that it affects the *homologous* chromosomes, leading to gametes with either an extra copy or a complete absence of that chromosome pair.
    - **Meiosis II:** Sister chromatids fail to separate during anaphase II. This results in two daughter cells with a normal number of chromosomes (n) and two daughter cells with an abnormal number (n+1 or n-1). Upon fertilization by a normal sperm, this leads to a mix of euploid (46,XX), disomic (47,XXX), and nullisomic (45,X) zygotes. The key feature of Meiosis II nondisjunction is that it affects *sister chromatids*, leading to aneuploidy for a *single* chromosome (e.g., an extra or missing X chromosome).
- **Patient's Case:** The patient is mosaic for 45,X and 46,XX cells. The mother is 46,XX and had miscarriages. The father is 46,XY. This pattern strongly suggests maternal origin. Meiosis I nondisjunction in the mother would produce eggs with either two X chromosomes (46,XX) or no X chromosomes (45,X). Fertilization by a normal sperm (23,X) would yield zygotes with two X chromosomes (46,XX) or one X chromosome (45,X). This perfectly explains the mosaicism observed in the patient.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Nondisjunction during meiosis II in the mother would produce eggs with either one X chromosome (45,X) or three X chromosomes (47,XXX). Fertilization by a normal sperm would yield zygotes with one X (45,X) or two X chromosomes (46,XX or 47,XXX). While 45,X cells are present, the presence of 46,XX cells *without* 47,XXX cells makes this less likely than Meiosis I nondisjunction. Meiosis II nondisjunction typically results in a mix of euploid, disomic, and nullisomic gametes, not just nullisomic and disomic ones in the offspring.
- (C): Nondisjunction during meiosis II in the father would produce sperm with either one X chromosome (45,X) or three X chromosomes (47,XXX). Fertilization of a normal egg (23,X) would yield zygotes with one X (45,X) or two X chromosomes (46,XX or 47,XXX). This is incorrect because the father is 46,XY, so his sperm should normally contain either an X or a Y chromosome. Meiosis II nondisjunction in the father is impossible for the X chromosome as he only has one X. Even if it were possible, the mother's normal karyotype (46,XX) makes maternal origin more likely given her history of miscarriages.
- (D): Mitotic nondisjunction occurs *after* fertilization, during early cell divisions of the zygote. This would lead to mosaicism, but typically involves autosomal trisomies (like mosaic Down syndrome) or sex chromosome aneuploidies. While it *could* theoretically explain 45,X/46,XX mosaicism if it occurred very early, it's less likely than meiotic nondisjunction, especially given the maternal history of miscarriages, which points towards an oogenesis defect. Mitotic nondisjunction usually results in cell lines with varying numbers of *all* chromosomes, not just a specific sex chromosome aneuploidy.
- (E): A translocation involving the X chromosome in the mother could lead to unbalanced gametes. For example, a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosome 21 and the X chromosome could lead to gametes with extra or missing X chromosomes. However, the patient's karyotype shows simple monosomy X (45,X) and disomy X (46,XX), not complex rearrangements. Also, the mother's karyotype was reported as normal, making a balanced translocation less likely, although not impossible without detailed cytogenetic analysis. The specific pattern of 45,X/46,XX mosaicism is most classically explained by Meiosis I nondisjunction.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Meiosis I nondisjunction affects homologous chromosomes, leading to gametes with either an extra copy (n+1) or a complete absence (n-1) of that chromosome pair. Fertilization results in zygotes with either trisomy (2n+1) or monosomy (2n-1). Meiosis II nondisjunction affects sister chromatids, leading to gametes with aneuploidy for a single chromosome (n+1 or n-1). Fertilization results in a mix of euploid, trisomic, and monosomic zygotes.
- **Differentials:** Consider Meiosis II nondisjunction (produces euploid, disomic, and nullisomic gametes), mitotic nondisjunction (occurs post-zygotically), and structural rearrangements (translocations, deletions, duplications) as causes of aneuploidy and mosaicism. The specific pattern of cell lines (45,X and 46,XX) and the maternal history strongly point towards Meiosis I nondisjunction in the mother.

<a id='question-031'></a>

### Question 031: Nondisjunction in Meiosis II
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Nondisjunction during meiosis II leads to aneuploidy in gametes, specifically resulting in 50% normal gametes, 25% gametes with an extra chromosome (disomic), and 25% gametes missing a chromosome (nullisomic). This contrasts with meiosis I nondisjunction, which produces 50% normal gametes and 50% aneuploid gametes (either +1 or -1 chromosome). (减数第二次分裂的不着丝粒点分离导致配子体细胞的染色体数目异常，产生50%正常的配子，25%多一个染色体的配子，和25%少一个染色体的配子。这与减数第一次分裂的不着丝粒点分离不同，后者产生50%正常的配子和50%染色体数目异常的配子（+1或-1）。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old woman, gravida 2, para 1, presents for routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Her previous pregnancy resulted in the spontaneous loss of a fetus at 10 weeks gestation. Amniocentesis is performed, and fetal karyotyping reveals 47,XX,+21 (Trisomy 21). The parents undergo karyotyping, which shows the mother to be phenotypically normal with a karyotype of 46,XX,t(14;21)(q21;q10) (Robertsonian translocation). The father is phenotypically normal with a karyotype of 46,XY. The mother is heterozygous for the translocation, carrying one normal chromosome 21 and one translocated chromosome 21 attached to chromosome 14. The couple is concerned about the recurrence risk in future pregnancies. Genetic counseling reveals that the mother's meiotic segregation pattern results in four types of gametes: normal (n=23), trisomic 21 (n=24, +21), monosomic 21 (n=22, -21), and a gamete with the translocation chromosome (n=23, t(14;21)). The risk of having another child with Trisomy 21 is significantly elevated. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the production of gametes with an extra chromosome 21 (n=24, +21) and gametes missing chromosome 21 (n=22, -21) by this mother?

(A) Nondisjunction of homologous chromosomes during Meiosis I.
(B) Nondisjunction of sister chromatids during Meiosis II.
(C) Premature separation of sister chromatids during Meiosis I.
(D) Failure of cytokinesis after Meiosis I.
(E) Crossing over between chromosome 14 and chromosome 21 during Prophase I.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old woman, previous miscarriage, current fetus with Trisomy 21.
- **Parental Karyotypes:** Mother is 46,XX,t(14;21), Father is 46,XY (normal). Mother is a carrier of a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21.
- **Fetal Karyotype:** 47,XX,+21 (Trisomy 21). This confirms the mother is the source of the extra chromosome 21.
- **Genetic Counseling:** Explains the mother's meiotic segregation pattern leads to four types of gametes: normal, trisomic 21, monosomic 21, and translocation carrier.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the production of both trisomic 21 (n=24, +21) and monosomic 21 (n=22, -21) gametes.
- **Key Clue:** The mother has a Robertsonian translocation t(14;21). During meiosis, the translocated chromosome (14;21) and the normal chromosome 21 pair with their homologs. This trivalent structure (normal 14, normal 21, t(14;21)) can segregate in multiple ways. The most common segregation pattern involves the normal 14, normal 21, and t(14;21) chromosomes aligning side-by-side. If the normal 14 and t(14;21) chromosomes segregate together to one pole and the normal 21 goes to the other pole, this results in a gamete with the translocation chromosome (n=23, t(14;21)) and a gamete missing chromosome 21 (n=22, -21). Alternatively, if the normal 14 and normal 21 segregate together to one pole and the t(14;21) goes to the other pole, this results in a gamete with the translocation chromosome (n=23, t(14;21)) and a gamete with two normal copies of chromosome 21 (n=24, +21). The production of monosomic and trisomic gametes requires the failure of sister chromatid separation during Meiosis II *after* the initial segregation event in Meiosis I. Specifically, if the normal chromosome 21 and the translocated chromosome (14;21) segregate to opposite poles in Meiosis I, each chromosome then replicates. In Meiosis II, if the sister chromatids of the normal chromosome 21 fail to separate, one gamete will receive two copies of the normal chromosome 21 (n=24, +21), and another gamete will receive no copies of chromosome 21 (n=22, -21). The same logic applies if the normal chromosome 14 and the translocated chromosome (14;21) segregate to opposite poles in Meiosis I.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Robertsonian Translocation:** Fusion of two acrocentric chromosomes (chromosomes with the centromere near one end) at the centromere, resulting in the loss of the short arms. t(14;21) is the most common type.
- **Meiosis I:** Homologous chromosomes pair and segregate. In the case of a t(14;21) carrier, a trivalent structure forms. The possible segregation patterns in Meiosis I are:
    1.  Normal 14 + Normal 21 -> Gamete 1 (n=23, normal)
        t(14;21) -> Gamete 2 (n=23, t(14;21))
        Result: 50% normal gametes, 50% translocation carrier gametes.
    2.  Normal 14 + t(14;21) -> Gamete 1 (n=23, t(14;21))
        Normal 21 -> Gamete 2 (n=23, +21)
        Result: 50% translocation carrier gametes, 50% trisomic 21 gametes.
    3.  Normal 21 + t(14;21) -> Gamete 1 (n=23, +21)
        Normal 14 -> Gamete 2 (n=23, -14)
        Result: 50% trisomic 21 gametes, 50% monosomic 14 gametes.
    The actual proportions are often different, but these are the possibilities. The most common outcome is ~50% normal, ~25% trisomic 21, ~25% translocation carrier. Monosomic 14 and monosomic 21 gametes are usually non-viable.
- **Meiosis II:** Sister chromatids separate. Nondisjunction in Meiosis II occurs when sister chromatids fail to separate. If this happens *after* the segregation of the normal 21 and the t(14;21) in Meiosis I, the resulting cells in Meiosis II will have either two copies of the normal 21 chromosome or zero copies of the normal 21 chromosome. This leads to the production of gametes with +21 (n=24, +21) and -21 (n=22, -21). The same logic applies to the segregation of the normal 14 and the t(14;21) in Meiosis I.
- **Conclusion:** The production of both trisomic 21 (n=24, +21) and monosomic 21 (n=22, -21) gametes requires nondisjunction of sister chromatids during Meiosis II.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Nondisjunction of homologous chromosomes during Meiosis I would lead to gametes with either +1 chromosome (e.g., +21) or -1 chromosome (e.g., -21) in 50% of cases, and normal gametes in 50% of cases. It does not directly explain the production of *both* trisomic and monosomic gametes simultaneously, nor does it account for the specific segregation pattern seen with Robertsonian translocations leading to translocation carrier gametes. This mechanism is characteristic of aneuploidy arising from errors in the first meiotic division (e.g., Klinefelter syndrome, Turner syndrome).
- (C): Premature separation of sister chromatids during Meiosis I (anaphase I) would lead to aneuploidy in the resulting daughter cells (and thus gametes) because each daughter cell would receive an incorrect number of chromosomes. This is essentially a form of nondisjunction in Meiosis I, leading to similar outcomes as described in (A) – 50% normal, 50% aneuploid gametes. It doesn't specifically explain the combination of trisomic and monosomic gametes arising from a translocation carrier.
- (D): Failure of cytokinesis after Meiosis I results in a single cell containing two diploid sets of chromosomes (heterokaryon). This cell would then undergo Meiosis II, potentially leading to aneuploid gametes, but the primary defect is in cell division, not chromosome segregation itself. While it can cause aneuploidy, it doesn't specifically explain the pattern of trisomic and monosomic gametes arising from the complex segregation of a Robertsonian translocation during Meiosis I followed by Meiosis II nondisjunction.
- (E): Crossing over between chromosome 14 and chromosome 21 during Prophase I is a normal event that occurs in heterozygotes for the translocation. It leads to the formation of a trivalent structure during Meiosis I. While the structure formed is crucial for the segregation errors, crossing over itself is not the *mechanism* causing the nondisjunction that leads to trisomic and monosomic gametes. Nondisjunction is the failure of chromosomes or chromatids to separate properly. Crossing over is the exchange of genetic material.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Meiosis II nondisjunction involves the failure of sister chromatids to separate, leading to gametes with either an extra copy

<a id='question-032'></a>

### Question 032: Maternal Age Effect on Nondisjunction: Prolonged Arrest of Oocytes in Dictyotene Phase (Prophase I) for Decades Degrading Cohesins - Clinical Scenario 2
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The increased risk of aneuploidy (specifically trisomy 21) in offspring born to older mothers is primarily due to the prolonged arrest of oocytes in the dictyotene stage of prophase I, leading to the degradation of cohesins necessary for proper chromosome segregation during meiosis I. (母亲年龄越大，后代染色体非整倍体（特别是21三体综合征）的风险越高，这主要是因为卵子在前期I的停滞期（dictyotene）中长期停滞，导致降解维持染色体正确分离所需的黏持蛋白。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 42-year-old primigravida (G1P0) presents for her first prenatal visit at 12 weeks gestation. Her medical history is unremarkable, and she denies any family history of genetic disorders or previous miscarriages. Routine prenatal screening reveals an elevated maternal serum alpha-fetoprotein (MSAFP) level of 2.5 MoM (multiples of the median) and a decreased unconjugated estriol (uE3) level of 0.7 MoM. Nuchal translucency (NT) measurement at 11 weeks was 3.5 mm (normal < 3.0 mm). Chorionic villus sampling (CVS) is performed, and karyotyping reveals a 47,XY,+21 mosaicism in the placental tissue (40% of cells). Amniocentesis is subsequently performed at 16 weeks, and amniotic fluid analysis shows 80% of fetal cells are 47,XY,+21. Given the maternal age and the prenatal screening results, the most likely underlying cellular mechanism contributing to the increased risk of this aneuploidy is:

(A) Failure of homologous chromosomes to pair during prophase I due to age-related decline in synaptonemal complex proteins.
(B) Premature separation of sister chromatids during anaphase II due to age-related defects in kinetochore-microtubule attachments.
(C) Impaired spindle assembly checkpoint (SAC) function leading to premature entry into anaphase I before proper bipolar attachment.
(D) Degradation of cohesin complexes holding sister chromatids together during the prolonged dictyotene arrest of oocytes, leading to chromosome missegregation in meiosis I.
(E) Increased rate of somatic mutations in the germline DNA repair pathways in older oocytes, leading to errors during DNA replication prior to meiosis.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Age (42 years):** Advanced maternal age is a major risk factor for aneuploidy, particularly trisomy 21 (Down syndrome). This immediately points towards mechanisms related to aging oocytes.
- **Prenatal Screening (Elevated MSAFP, Decreased uE3, Increased NT):** These findings are suggestive of an increased risk for aneuploidy, specifically trisomy 21. Elevated MSAFP and decreased uE3 are classic markers, and increased NT is associated with chromosomal abnormalities.
- **Karyotype Results (Mosaicism):** The mosaicism (40% placental cells 47,XY,+21, 80% fetal cells 47,XY,+21) indicates a chromosomal abnormality. The presence of trisomy 21 in the fetus confirms the suspicion raised by screening. The mosaicism itself can occur due to post-zygotic mitotic nondisjunction, but the *increased risk* is primarily linked to meiotic nondisjunction in the oocyte.
- **Diagnosis (Trisomy 21):** The specific aneuploidy is trisomy 21, which is strongly associated with maternal age effect.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *underlying cellular mechanism* contributing to the *increased risk* of aneuploidy in older mothers.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Oocyte Aging:** Oocytes are arrested in the dictyotene stage of prophase I during fetal development and remain arrested until ovulation, potentially for decades.
- **Cohesin Degradation:** During this prolonged arrest, the cohesin complexes that hold sister chromatids together begin to degrade. Cohesins are crucial for maintaining chromosome integrity and proper alignment on the metaphase plate.
- **Meiosis I Nondisjunction:** In younger women, cohesins are largely intact when meiosis I resumes. However, in older women, the degradation of cohesins is more advanced. This weakened cohesion makes it difficult for homologous chromosomes (in meiosis I) or sister chromatids (in meiosis II) to segregate properly. The weakened connection increases the likelihood of chromosomes failing to separate correctly (nondisjunction) during anaphase I or anaphase II.
- **Trisomy 21 Mechanism:** Nondisjunction during meiosis I leads to gametes with either two copies or no copies of chromosome 21. Fertilization with a normal gamete results in trisomy 21 (two copies from the egg, one from the sperm) or monosomy 21 (no copies from the egg, one from the sperm). Nondisjunction in meiosis II leads to gametes with either one copy or three copies of chromosome 21. Fertilization with a normal gamete results in trisomy 21 (one copy from the egg, two from the sperm) or monosomy 21 (three copies from the egg, one from the sperm). The risk of nondisjunction increases significantly with maternal age, correlating with the duration of dictyotene arrest and cohesin degradation.
- **Kaplan/First Aid Relevance:** This mechanism is a classic example of the maternal age effect on aneuploidy, directly linking oocyte aging, prolonged dictyotene arrest, cohesin degradation, and increased risk of meiotic nondisjunction.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Failure of homologous chromosomes to pair during prophase I due to age-related decline in synaptonemal complex proteins.** While synaptonemal complex proteins are important for synapsis (pairing), the primary defect associated with maternal age is not failure of pairing but rather the *loss of cohesion* after pairing has occurred. Pairing defects can occur but are less directly linked to the age effect on trisomy 21 risk compared to cohesin degradation.
- (B): **Premature separation of sister chromatids during anaphase II due to age-related defects in kinetochore-microtubule attachments.** Defects in kinetochore-microtubule attachments can cause nondisjunction, but this is more related to the spindle assembly checkpoint (SAC) and overall mitotic/meiotic fidelity, not specifically the prolonged arrest and cohesin degradation characteristic of aging oocytes. Nondisjunction in meiosis I is more common than meiosis II, and the primary age-related defect is cohesin loss *before* anaphase.
- (C): **Impaired spindle assembly checkpoint (SAC) function leading to premature entry into anaphase I before proper bipolar attachment.** A defective SAC would lead to aneuploidy, but the primary mechanism linked to maternal age is the weakening of cohesion due to prolonged arrest, not a primary defect in the SAC itself. While SAC function might decline with age, it's not considered the main driver of the increased risk.
- (E): **Increased rate of somatic mutations in the germline DNA repair pathways in older oocytes, leading to errors during DNA replication prior to meiosis.** While DNA repair mechanisms can decline with age, increasing mutation rates, this primarily contributes to genomic instability and potentially cancer risk, not directly to the *nondisjunction* events leading to aneuploidy like trisomy 21. The core issue in maternal age effect is the mechanical failure of chromosome segregation due to cohesin loss.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The increased risk of aneuploidy (especially trisomy 21) with advanced maternal age is primarily due to the prolonged arrest of oocytes in dictyotene (prophase I) leading to the degradation of cohesin complexes, which compromises chromosome segregation during meiosis I. This is a key concept in medical genetics. Differential diagnoses for aneuploidy include paternal age effect (associated with specific mutations like Apert syndrome, achondroplasia), translocation carriers, and Robertsonian translocations. However, the question specifically asks for the mechanism related to *maternal age*.

---

<a id='question-033'></a>

### Question 033: Down Syndrome (Trisomy 21 / 47,XX,+21)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understand the clinical features, cytogenetic basis (nondisjunction), and associated biochemical/molecular changes in Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), specifically linking the extra chromosome 21 to increased expression of genes located on it, such as APP, leading to amyloid beta accumulation and early-onset Alzheimer's disease. (理解21三体综合征的临床特征、细胞遗传学基础（不分离）以及相关的生化/分子变化，特别是将21号染色体上的额外染色体与位于其上的基因（如APP）的表达增加联系起来，导致淀粉样β积累和早期阿尔茨海默病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman delivers a full-term infant. The pregnancy was uneventful, but the mother's age is noted to be a risk factor. The newborn presents with characteristic facial features including upslanting palpebral fissures, a flattened facial profile, epicanthal folds, and a single transverse palmar crease (Simian crease). Cardiac examination reveals a ventricular septal defect (VSD). A karyotype analysis is performed, revealing 47,XX,+21. The infant is diagnosed with Down syndrome. At age 35, the patient begins to exhibit progressive memory loss, confusion, and behavioral changes. Brain imaging shows significant cerebral atrophy, particularly in the hippocampus and cortex. Cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis reveals elevated levels of amyloid-beta 42 (Aβ42) peptide and decreased levels of phosphorylated tau protein. A brain biopsy confirms the presence of neuritic plaques and neurofibrillary tangles, characteristic of Alzheimer's disease.

Which of the following molecular mechanisms BEST explains the early onset of Alzheimer's disease in this patient with Down syndrome?

(A) Increased expression of presenilin-1 (PSEN1) gene on chromosome 14, leading to enhanced γ-secretase activity and increased production of Aβ42.
(B) Decreased expression of the amyloid precursor protein (APP) gene on chromosome 21, resulting in reduced Aβ42 production and subsequent neuronal damage.
(C) Increased expression of the amyloid precursor protein (APP) gene on chromosome 21 due to trisomy, leading to increased production and accumulation of Aβ42.
(D) Mutations in the apolipoprotein E (APOE) gene on chromosome 19, specifically the ε4 allele, which impairs Aβ42 clearance from the brain.
(E) Decreased activity of neprilysin (NEP), an Aβ42-degrading enzyme, due to oxidative stress caused by mitochondrial dysfunction associated with Down syndrome.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Newborn with classic Down syndrome features (facial dysmorphism, Simian crease, VSD) confirmed by karyotype (47,XX,+21). This establishes the diagnosis of Trisomy 21.
- **Later Presentation:** Patient develops symptoms of Alzheimer's disease (memory loss, confusion) at a relatively young age (35).
- **Diagnostic Findings:** Brain imaging shows atrophy, CSF shows elevated Aβ42 and decreased phosphorylated tau, and biopsy confirms Alzheimer's pathology (plaques and tangles). This confirms early-onset Alzheimer's disease.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism linking Trisomy 21 to early-onset Alzheimer's disease.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Down syndrome is caused by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 21.
- The amyloid precursor protein (APP) gene is located on chromosome 21.
- Having three copies of the APP gene (trisomy) leads to increased transcription and translation of the APP protein.
- APP is processed by secretases (α, β, and γ) to produce various peptides, including amyloid-beta (Aβ).
- The amyloidogenic pathway involves sequential cleavage by β-secretase and γ-secretase, producing Aβ peptides, primarily Aβ40 and Aβ42.
- Aβ42 is particularly prone to aggregation and is the main component of the amyloid plaques found in Alzheimer's disease.
- Increased APP expression in Down syndrome leads to increased production of Aβ42.
- This excess Aβ42 accumulates in the brain, forming plaques earlier and more extensively than in sporadic Alzheimer's disease, leading to the characteristic early onset of Alzheimer's pathology in individuals with Down syndrome.
- The decreased phosphorylated tau in CSF is also consistent with Alzheimer's disease, as tau hyperphosphorylation leads to the formation of neurofibrillary tangles.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Presenilin-1 (PSEN1) is located on chromosome 14. Mutations in PSEN1 (and PSEN2 on chromosome 1) are known causes of *familial* early-onset Alzheimer's disease, affecting γ-secretase activity. While γ-secretase activity is relevant, the primary mechanism in Down syndrome is the *overexpression* of APP due to the extra chromosome 21 copy, not a mutation in PSEN1 on chromosome 14. This distractor confuses the genetic locus of APP with PSEN1.
- (B): This option incorrectly states that APP expression is *decreased*. In Down syndrome, the presence of an extra chromosome 21 leads to *increased* expression of all genes located on that chromosome, including APP. Decreased APP expression would lead to *less* Aβ42 production, not the increased production seen in Down syndrome-associated Alzheimer's. This tests the understanding of gene dosage effect in trisomy.
- (D): Apolipoprotein E (APOE) gene is on chromosome 19. The APOE ε4 allele is a major genetic risk factor for *late-onset* sporadic Alzheimer's disease, primarily by affecting Aβ42 clearance and aggregation. While APOE status can influence Alzheimer's risk and progression, it is not the primary mechanism causing the *early onset* specifically linked to the trisomy 21 condition itself. The core issue in Down syndrome is the increased APP production due to the extra chromosome.
- (E): Neprilysin (NEP) is an enzyme that degrades Aβ. Decreased NEP activity could contribute to Aβ accumulation. While oxidative stress and mitochondrial dysfunction can occur in Down syndrome, and NEP activity might be affected, the *primary* and most direct cause of increased Aβ42 accumulation leading to early Alzheimer's in Down syndrome is the overexpression of APP due to the extra copy of chromosome 21. This distractor focuses on a secondary potential mechanism rather than the fundamental genetic cause.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) leads to increased expression of genes on chromosome 21, most notably APP. This overexpression results in increased production of Aβ42, leading to early-onset Alzheimer's disease pathology. Key differentials for early-onset Alzheimer's include mutations in APP, PSEN1, or PSEN2, but in Down syndrome, the mechanism is gene dosage (trisomy) of APP.

---

<a id='question-034'></a>

### Question 034: Down Syndrome Craniofacial Features
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The characteristic craniofacial features of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) are caused by the overexpression of genes located on chromosome 21, including those involved in craniofacial development, such as *DYRK1A* (Dual-specificity tyrosine-phosphorylation-regulated kinase 1A), which affects neural development and cell growth. (唐氏综合征 (21三体综合征) 的典型颅面部特征是由 21 号染色体上基因的过表达引起的，包括参与颅面部发育的基因，例如 *DYRK1A*，它会影响神经发育和细胞生长。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is brought to the pediatrician for a well-child check. The mother, a 38-year-old primigravida, reports an uneventful pregnancy and delivery. Physical examination reveals several distinct features: a flattened facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, a depressed nasal bridge, and small, low-set ears. Additionally, a ring of small white spots (Brushfield spots) is noted in the periphery of both irises. The infant also exhibits hypotonia and a single transverse palmar crease (simian crease) on the right hand. Karyotyping performed on the infant confirms the diagnosis. Which of the following molecular mechanisms is most directly responsible for the characteristic craniofacial features observed in this infant?

(A) Haploinsufficiency of the *PAX6* gene on chromosome 11, leading to impaired eye development.
(B) Overexpression of the *DYRK1A* gene on chromosome 21, disrupting normal craniofacial morphogenesis.
(C) Deletion of the *TBX5* gene on chromosome 9, resulting in limb and cardiac abnormalities.
(D) Mutation in the *FGFR3* gene on chromosome 4, causing premature fusion of cranial sutures.
(E) Trisomy 18, leading to characteristic features like clenched fists, rocker-bottom feet, and severe intellectual disability.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-day-old female infant with distinct craniofacial features (flattened facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, depressed nasal bridge, Brushfield spots, small/low-set ears), hypotonia, and a simian crease.
- **Maternal History:** 38-year-old primigravida (advanced maternal age is a risk factor for chromosomal aneuploidy).
- **Diagnostic Test:** Karyotyping confirms the diagnosis. The constellation of features strongly suggests Down syndrome (Trisomy 21).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism responsible for the *craniofacial features* specifically.
- **Key Clues:** The specific craniofacial features (flattened profile, upslanting fissures, epicanthal folds, depressed nasal bridge, Brushfield spots) are hallmarks of Down syndrome. The diagnosis is confirmed by karyotyping, implying an extra copy of chromosome 21. The question asks for the mechanism linking the extra chromosome 21 to these specific features.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- Down syndrome is caused by trisomy 21, meaning there are three copies of chromosome 21 instead of the usual two.
- This leads to overexpression of all genes located on chromosome 21.
- Several genes on chromosome 21 are implicated in the development of Down syndrome phenotypes, particularly the craniofacial features.
- *DYRK1A* (Dual-specificity tyrosine-phosphorylation-regulated kinase 1A) is located on chromosome 21 (21q21.3).
- *DYRK1A* encodes a protein kinase involved in regulating cell growth, differentiation, and apoptosis. It plays a crucial role in brain development and craniofacial morphogenesis.
- Overexpression of *DYRK1A* due to the extra copy of chromosome 21 is thought to contribute significantly to the characteristic craniofacial features of Down syndrome, including the flattened facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, and depressed nasal bridge, possibly by interfering with normal neural crest cell migration and differentiation, which are critical for facial development.
- Other genes on chromosome 21, like *APP* (Amyloid Precursor Protein) and *SOD1* (Superoxide Dismutase 1), are also overexpressed but are more directly linked to Alzheimer's disease pathology and cellular stress, respectively, rather than the primary cause of the craniofacial dysmorphology.
- Therefore, the overexpression of *DYRK1A* is considered a major contributor to the specific craniofacial phenotype described.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Haploinsufficiency of *PAX6* on chromosome 11 causes aniridia (absence of the iris) and other eye abnormalities. This gene is crucial for eye development, but its deficiency, not overexpression on chromosome 21, causes eye defects. The infant has Brushfield spots, not aniridia.
- (C): Deletion of the *TBX5* gene on chromosome 9 causes Holt-Oram syndrome, characterized by upper limb abnormalities (radial ray defects) and congenital heart defects. This is unrelated to Down syndrome or its craniofacial features.
- (D): Mutations in the *FGFR3* gene on chromosome 4 cause achondroplasia (a form of dwarfism) and other skeletal dysplasias, characterized by impaired endochondral ossification and premature fusion of cranial sutures (craniosynostosis). This is unrelated to Down syndrome.
- (E): Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) is a different chromosomal aneuploidy. It is characterized by severe intellectual disability, growth retardation, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects. While it involves a chromosomal abnormality, it is not Down syndrome and presents with a distinct set of features.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- The characteristic craniofacial features of Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) are primarily attributed to the overexpression of genes located on chromosome 21, notably *DYRK1A*. (唐氏综合征 (21三体综合征) 的典型颅面部特征主要归因于 21 号染色体上基因的过表达，特别是 *DYRK1A*。)
- Key differentials for chromosomal abnormalities causing dysmorphic features include Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome) and Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome), each with distinct phenotypes. Other genetic syndromes like Holt-Oram (TBX5 deletion) and achondroplasia (FGFR3 mutation) involve specific gene defects unrelated to chromosome 21 aneuploidy. (导致面部畸形的染色体异常的关键鉴别诊断包括 18 三体综合征 (爱德华兹综合征) 和 13 三体综合征 (帕陶综合征)，每种都有不同的表型。 其他遗传综合征，如 Holt-Oram 综合征 (TBX5 缺失) 和软骨发育不全 (FGFR3 突变)，涉及与 21 号染色体单倍体无关的特定基因缺陷。)

<a id='question-035'></a>

### Question 035: Down Syndrome Extremity Findings
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the recognition of characteristic physical findings associated with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), specifically focusing on extremity abnormalities like the single transverse palmar crease (simian crease), clinodactyly of the fifth finger, and a wide sandal gap. It assesses the ability to link these phenotypic features to the underlying genetic cause. (本题考察唐氏综合征（21三体综合征）相关的特征性体格发现，特别是四肢的异常，如单横掌纹（猴纹）、第五指弯曲畸形和第一、二趾间宽间隙。考察将这些表型特征与潜在的遗传原因联系起来的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-day-old female infant is brought to the pediatrician for a well-child check. The mother, a 38-year-old primigravida, reports an uneventful pregnancy except for advanced maternal age. Physical examination reveals hypotonia, a flattened facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, and epicanthal folds. Examination of the hands shows a single transverse palmar crease on both palms. The fifth finger on the right hand exhibits clinodactyly (inward curvature). Examination of the feet reveals a wide space between the first and second toes (sandal gap). Karyotyping performed on the infant confirms the diagnosis.

Which of the following genetic abnormalities is most likely responsible for the constellation of findings observed in this infant?

(A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18
(B) Trisomy 13
(C) Translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21
(D) Monosomy X
(E) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 17

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 3-day-old female infant, mother is 38 years old (advanced maternal age). Advanced maternal age is a known risk factor for chromosomal aneuploidies.
- **Clinical Findings:** Hypotonia, flattened facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds. These are classic dysmorphic features associated with Down syndrome.
- **Extremity Findings:** Single transverse palmar crease (simian crease) on both palms, clinodactyly of the fifth finger (right hand), wide sandal gap between the first and second toes. These are characteristic skeletal and hand/foot abnormalities frequently seen in Down syndrome.
- **Diagnostic Test:** Karyotyping confirms the diagnosis. This implies a chromosomal abnormality.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely genetic abnormality responsible for the findings.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- The clinical presentation (dysmorphic features, hypotonia, specific extremity findings) strongly suggests Down syndrome.
- Down syndrome is most commonly caused by Trisomy 21, meaning there are three copies of chromosome 21 instead of the usual two.
- Trisomy 21 can arise from nondisjunction during meiosis (leading to a gamete with an extra chromosome 21), resulting in a karyotype of 47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21.
- However, Down syndrome can also be caused by a Robertsonian translocation, where a portion or the entire chromosome 21 attaches to another chromosome, most commonly chromosome 14. This results in a karyotype like 46,XX,der(14;21)(q10;q10) or 46,XY,der(14;21)(q10;q10). Although the total chromosome number is 46, the individual has three copies of the critical region of chromosome 21.
- The question asks for the *genetic abnormality* responsible. The vignette describes classic Down syndrome features. While Trisomy 21 is the most common cause (~95%), translocation is the next most common (~4%). The question doesn't specify the karyotype result, only that karyotyping confirmed the diagnosis. Both Trisomy 21 and the translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21 lead to the same clinical phenotype (Down syndrome). Option (C) specifically describes a translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21, which is a known cause of Down syndrome. Option (A) describes Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18), Option (B) describes Patau syndrome (Trisomy 13), Option (D) describes Turner syndrome (Monosomy X), and Option (E) describes a duplication of chromosome 17, none of which present with this specific constellation of features characteristic of Down syndrome. Given the options, a translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21 is a valid genetic cause for the described phenotype.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deletion of the short arm of chromosome 18: This describes Edwards syndrome (Trisomy 18), characterized by severe intellectual disability, micrognathia, clenched fists with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and cardiac defects. The features described in the vignette (flattened face, upslanting eyes, simian crease, sandal gap) are not typical of Trisomy 18.
- (B) Trisomy 13: This describes Patau syndrome, characterized by severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, cleft lip/palate, polydactyly, rocker-bottom feet, and cardiac defects. The features described in the vignette are not typical of Trisomy 13.
- (D) Monosomy X: This describes Turner syndrome (45,X), affecting females. Features include short stature, webbed neck, broad chest with widely spaced nipples, ovarian dysgenesis, and cardiac defects (e.g., coarctation of the aorta). The features described in the vignette are not typical of Turner syndrome.
- (E) Duplication of the long arm of chromosome 17: Duplications of chromosome 17q are rare and associated with variable phenotypes, often including developmental delay and dysmorphic features, but not the specific combination seen in Down syndrome (flattened facies, upslanting palpebral fissures, simian crease, sandal gap, clinodactyly).

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Down syndrome (Trisomy 21) is characterized by specific dysmorphic features and physical findings, including hypotonia, flattened facial profile, upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, single transverse palmar crease, clinodactyly, and a wide sandal gap. It can be caused by nondisjunction (Trisomy 21) or Robertsonian translocation (most commonly involving chromosomes 14 and 21). Key differentials include other trisomies (Edwards syndrome - Trisomy 18; Patau syndrome - Trisomy 13) and sex chromosome aneuploidies (Turner syndrome - Monosomy X). Recognizing the specific constellation of findings is crucial for diagnosis.
