<a id='question-046'></a>

### Question 046: Polyploidy (Triploidy 69,XXX/XXY vs Tetraploidy 92,XXXX): Dispermy or Polar Body Retention, Early Spontaneous Abortions, Partial Hydatidiform Mole - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the genetic mechanisms leading to different forms of polyploidy (triploidy vs. tetraploidy) in humans, specifically relating them to fertilization events (dispermy vs. polar body retention) and associated clinical outcomes (spontaneous abortion vs. partial molar pregnancy). (本题考察人类多倍体（三倍体 vs 四倍体）的遗传机制，特别是它们与受精事件（双精子受精 vs 极体滞留）以及相关临床结果（流产 vs 部分葡萄胎）的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman, G3P1A1, presents for her first prenatal visit at 8 weeks gestation. Her previous pregnancy resulted in a spontaneous abortion at 12 weeks. Her current pregnancy is progressing normally based on ultrasound, showing a fetus with a crown-rump length consistent with gestational age. However, the ultrasound reveals several abnormalities: a single umbilical artery, cystic hygroma, and clenched hands. Chorionic villus sampling (CVS) is performed, and karyotyping of the fetal cells reveals a chromosomal constitution of 69,XXY. The mother's karyotype is normal (46,XX). The father's karyotype is also normal (46,XY). The patient is counseled regarding the prognosis.

Which of the following mechanisms is the MOST likely cause of the observed fetal karyotype?

(A) Dispermy followed by mitotic failure in a diploid oocyte.
(B) Fertilization of a diploid oocyte by a haploid sperm, followed by mitotic failure.
(C) Fertilization of a diploid oocyte by a diploid sperm.
(D) Failure of the second meiotic division in the oocyte, resulting in a diploid oocyte, followed by fertilization by a haploid sperm.
(E) Failure of the first meiotic division in the oocyte, resulting in a diploid oocyte, followed by fertilization by a haploid sperm.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient History:** 32-year-old G3P1A1, previous spontaneous abortion. This suggests a potential underlying genetic issue affecting pregnancy viability.
*   **Current Pregnancy:** 8 weeks gestation, ultrasound shows fetus consistent with gestational age but with multiple anomalies (single umbilical artery, cystic hygroma, clenched hands). These are common features associated with trisomies and polyploidy.
*   **CVS Karyotype:** 69,XXY. This indicates triploidy, meaning the fetus has three sets of chromosomes instead of the usual two. The sex chromosome complement (XXY) indicates the extra set came from the father (Y chromosome).
*   **Parental Karyotypes:** Both parents are normal (46,XX and 46,XY). This rules out parental chromosomal abnormalities as the primary cause of the fetal triploidy.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism causing the 69,XXY karyotype.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
*   **Normal Fertilization:** A diploid oocyte (23 chromosomes) is fertilized by a haploid sperm (23 chromosomes), resulting in a diploid zygote (46 chromosomes).
*   **Triploidy (69 chromosomes):** Occurs when a diploid zygote receives an extra set of chromosomes (23 chromosomes). This can happen in two main ways:
    *   **Dispermy:** Two sperm fertilize a normal haploid oocyte. This results in a zygote with 23 (oocyte) + 23 (sperm 1) + 23 (sperm 2) = 69 chromosomes. The sex chromosome complement depends on the sex chromosomes of the two sperm (e.g., XXY, XYY, or XXX).
    *   **Polar Body Retention:** The oocyte completes meiosis I but fails to extrude the second polar body during meiosis II. This results in a diploid oocyte (23 chromosomes from mother x 2 sets) which is then fertilized by a haploid sperm (23 chromosomes). This results in a zygote with 23 (oocyte set 1) + 23 (oocyte set 2) + 23 (sperm) = 69 chromosomes. The sex chromosome complement of the oocyte is XX, so the resulting triploid will be XXX or XXY (depending on the sperm's sex chromosome).
*   **Tetraploidy (92 chromosomes):** Occurs when a diploid zygote receives two extra sets of chromosomes. This typically happens when a diploid oocyte (due to failure of meiosis II) is fertilized by a diploid sperm (due to failure of meiosis I or II). This results in a zygote with 23 (oocyte set 1) + 23 (oocyte set 2) + 23 (sperm set 1) + 23 (sperm set 2) = 92 chromosomes. Tetraploidy is usually lethal early in development.
*   **Analyzing the Case:** The fetus has a 69,XXY karyotype. This means the extra set of chromosomes came from the father (Y chromosome). This rules out dispermy as the sole mechanism, as dispermy would result in XXY, XYY, or XXX, but the extra set must originate from the sperm. It also rules out polar body retention, as the oocyte contributes XX chromosomes, and the sperm contributes the Y chromosome, leading to XXY. Therefore, the mechanism must involve a diploid oocyte (resulting from failure of the second meiotic division) being fertilized by a haploid sperm.
*   **Option (D):** Failure of the second meiotic division in the oocyte results in a diploid oocyte (23 chromosomes, XX). Fertilization by a haploid sperm (23 chromosomes, X or Y) results in a triploid zygote (69 chromosomes, XXX or XXY). This matches the observed 69,XXY karyotype.
*   **Option (A):** Dispermy (two sperm fertilizing a haploid oocyte) results in 69 chromosomes. If the oocyte is XX and the sperm are X and Y, the result is 69,XXY. However, dispermy is less common than errors in meiosis II of the oocyte. Also, the question asks for the *most likely* mechanism.
*   **Option (B):** Fertilization of a diploid oocyte by a haploid sperm. This is essentially the same mechanism as (D), but (D) specifies *how* the oocyte became diploid (failure of the second meiotic division), which is the most common cause of diploid oocytes.
*   **Option (C):** Fertilization of a diploid oocyte by a diploid sperm. This leads to tetraploidy (92 chromosomes), not triploidy.
*   **Option (E):** Failure of the first meiotic division in the oocyte results in a diploid oocyte (23 chromosomes, XX). Fertilization by a haploid sperm (23 chromosomes, X or Y) results in a triploid zygote (69 chromosomes, XXX or XXY). While possible, failure of the second meiotic division (meiosis II) is more common than failure of the first meiotic division (meiosis I) in human oocytes, making option (D) the *most likely* cause.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Dispermy (two sperm fertilizing a normal haploid oocyte) can lead to triploidy (69 chromosomes). For example, if a haploid oocyte (23,X) is fertilized by two sperm (23,X and 23,Y), the result is 69,XXY. However, the most common cause of triploidy is the failure of the second meiotic division in the oocyte, leading to a diploid oocyte (23,XX) which is then fertilized by a haploid sperm (23,Y). While dispermy is a possible mechanism, it is less frequent than polar body retention or failure of meiosis II. This option is plausible but not the *most likely*.
- (B): Fertilization of a diploid oocyte by a haploid sperm leads to triploidy (69 chromosomes). This statement is correct in principle, but it doesn't specify *how* the oocyte became diploid. Option (D) provides the specific mechanism (failure of the second meiotic division), which is the most common cause of diploid oocytes leading to triploidy. Therefore, (D) is more precise and accurate as the *most likely* cause.
- (C): Fertilization of a diploid oocyte (from failure of meiosis II, 23,XX) by a diploid sperm (from failure of meiosis I or II, 23,XY) results in tetraploidy (92 chromosomes, 46,XXYY). This is incorrect because the observed karyotype is triploidy (69,XXY), not tetraploidy.
- (E): Failure of the first meiotic division in the oocyte results in a diploid oocyte (23,XX). Fertilization by a haploid sperm (23,Y) results in a triploid zygote (69,XXY). This is a possible mechanism for triploidy. However, errors in the second meiotic division (leading to polar body retention or failure of polar body extrusion) are more common causes of diploid oocytes than errors in the first meiotic division. Therefore, option (D) is considered the *most likely* mechanism.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Triploidy (69 chromosomes) most commonly arises from fertilization of a diploid oocyte (due to failure of the second meiotic division, resulting in polar body retention) by a haploid sperm, or less commonly, from dispermy (fertilization of a haploid oocyte by two sperm). Tetraploidy (92 chromosomes) results from fertilization of a diploid oocyte by a diploid sperm. These errors in meiosis or fertilization lead to aneuploidy or polyploidy, often resulting in non-viable pregnancies or conditions like partial hydatidiform mole. Key differentials include monosomy X (Turner syndrome, 45,X) resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis I or II, and trisomies (e.g., Down syndrome, 47,XX,+21) resulting from nondisjunction in meiosis I or II. Understanding the specific chromosomal complement (e.g., 69,XXY) allows deduction of the underlying mechanism.

<a id='question-047'></a>

### Question 047: Aneuploidy Concept: Deviation from Diploid Number (2n = 46) by Addition or Loss of Specific Individual Chromosomes - Clinical Scenario 3
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the clinical features and underlying chromosomal abnormalities associated with specific aneuploidies, particularly trisomies and monosomies, and differentiating them based on phenotypic presentation and karyotype analysis. (理解特定染色体非整倍体，特别是三体和单体，相关的临床特征和潜在的核型分析，并根据表型和核型区分它们。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old G3P2 woman presents for routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Her previous two pregnancies resulted in healthy full-term infants. Her current pregnancy is complicated by advanced maternal age. Nuchal translucency screening at 12 weeks was elevated (95th percentile). Quad screen results show elevated alpha-fetoprotein (AFP) and decreased unconjugated estriol (uE3). Amniocentesis is performed, and amniotic fluid is sent for karyotyping. The karyotype reveals 47,XX,+21. Ultrasound examination shows a single umbilical artery, increased nuchal fold thickness, shortened femur length, and cardiac defects (specifically, an endocardial cushion defect). The mother is counseled regarding the findings. Which of the following is the MOST likely long-term neurodevelopmental outcome for this fetus?

(A) Intellectual disability characterized by severe cognitive impairment, microcephaly, and early-onset seizures.
(B) Intellectual disability characterized by mild to moderate cognitive impairment, characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat facial profile), and increased risk of early-onset Alzheimer's disease.
(C) Intellectual disability characterized by severe cognitive impairment, hypotonia, and congenital heart defects, often associated with a deletion on the short arm of chromosome 22.
(D) Intellectual disability characterized by moderate cognitive impairment, tall stature, long limbs (dolichostenomelia), and characteristic facial features (elfin facies).
(E) Intellectual disability characterized by severe cognitive impairment, microcephaly, seizures, and characteristic hand movements (hand-wringing, hand-mouthing).

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Advanced maternal age (35 years):** A known risk factor for chromosomal aneuploidies, particularly trisomy 21.
*   **Elevated nuchal translucency:** A marker for increased risk of chromosomal abnormalities, including trisomy 21.
*   **Elevated AFP and decreased uE3:** These quad screen results are characteristic findings associated with trisomy 21.
*   **Karyotype 47,XX,+21:** Confirms the diagnosis of Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) in a female fetus.
*   **Ultrasound findings (single umbilical artery, increased nuchal fold, shortened femur, cardiac defects):** These are common physical findings associated with Trisomy 21. The endocardial cushion defect is particularly characteristic.
*   **Question asks for long-term neurodevelopmental outcome:** This directs the focus to the cognitive and neurological consequences of Trisomy 21.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
Trisomy 21, also known as Down syndrome, is caused by the presence of an extra copy of chromosome 21. This leads to overexpression of genes located on chromosome 21. The gene *APP* (Amyloid Precursor Protein) is located on chromosome 21. Overexpression of *APP* leads to increased production of amyloid-beta peptides, which aggregate to form amyloid plaques, a hallmark of Alzheimer's disease. Individuals with Down syndrome have a significantly increased risk of developing early-onset Alzheimer's disease, often presenting in their 50s or 60s. The neurodevelopmental outcome typically involves intellectual disability, ranging from mild to moderate cognitive impairment. Characteristic facial features (upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, flat facial profile) are also common. The clinical presentation described in the vignette (maternal age, screening results, karyotype, ultrasound findings) definitively points to Trisomy 21. Therefore, the expected long-term neurodevelopmental outcome is intellectual disability (mild to moderate) and an increased risk of early-onset Alzheimer's disease.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): This describes features more consistent with Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome). Trisomy 18 is associated with severe cognitive impairment, microcephaly, clenched hands with overlapping fingers, rocker-bottom feet, and congenital heart defects. The karyotype would be 47,XX,+18 or 47,XY,+18. The specific features mentioned (severe cognitive impairment, microcephaly, early seizures) and the implied karyotype are incorrect for Trisomy 21.
- (C): This describes features more consistent with DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome). This syndrome is caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 22, not a trisomy. It is associated with congenital heart defects (conotruncal defects), immune deficiency (thymic hypoplasia), hypocalcemia (parathyroid hypoplasia), characteristic facial features, and developmental delay/intellectual disability. The karyotype would typically be 46,XX or 46,XY, but FISH or microarray would show the 22q11.2 deletion. The specific features (severe cognitive impairment, hypotonia, deletion on 22q short arm) are incorrect for Trisomy 21.
- (D): This describes features more consistent with Williams syndrome. Williams syndrome is caused by a microdeletion on chromosome 7 (7q11.23). It is associated with moderate cognitive impairment, characteristic "elfin" facial features, cardiovascular problems (supravalvular aortic stenosis), hypercalcemia in infancy, and a distinctive personality profile (overly friendly, anxious). The karyotype would typically be 46,XX or 46,XY, but FISH or microarray would show the 7q11.23 deletion. The specific features (moderate cognitive impairment, tall stature, long limbs, elfin facies) are incorrect for Trisomy 21.
- (E): This describes features more consistent with Angelman syndrome. Angelman syndrome is typically caused by a deletion or mutation affecting the maternal copy of the *UBE3A* gene on chromosome 15q11-q13. It is characterized by severe intellectual disability, microcephaly, seizures, ataxia, characteristic happy demeanor with frequent laughter, and specific motor behaviors (hand-wringing, hand-mouthing). The karyotype is usually normal (46,XX or 46,XY), but molecular testing reveals the specific genetic defect. The specific features (severe cognitive impairment, microcephaly, seizures, hand movements) are incorrect for Trisomy 21.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) is characterized by an extra copy of chromosome 21, leading to specific physical features, congenital anomalies (especially cardiac defects), and intellectual disability (typically mild to moderate). A key long-term complication is an increased risk of early-onset Alzheimer's disease due to *APP* gene overexpression. Differentiating Trisomy 21 from other aneuploidies like Trisomy 18 (Edwards syndrome), Trisomy 13 (Patau syndrome), and microdeletion syndromes (like DiGeorge, Williams, Angelman) requires careful attention to specific clinical features, screening results, and karyotype findings.

<a id='question-048'></a>

### Question 48: Autosomal Monosomy
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Autosomal monosomy (loss of a complete autosome) is incompatible with life due to haploinsufficiency of essential genes, whereas sex monosomy (e.g., Turner syndrome, 45,X) is survivable because the single X chromosome can be inactivated and the Y chromosome carries few essential genes. (染色体单体缺失：常染色体单体缺失（如45,XX,-21）由于必需基因的半合子不足而与生命不相容，而性染色体单体缺失（如45,X）是可以存活的，因为唯一的X染色体可以失活，并且Y染色体携带的必需基因很少。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old woman presents to a genetics clinic after experiencing two consecutive spontaneous miscarriages in the first trimester. She and her husband have undergone extensive infertility workup, including karyotyping. The woman's karyotype reveals a normal 46,XX pattern. Her husband's karyotype shows a balanced reciprocal translocation between chromosomes 21 and 22, denoted as t(21;22)(q10;q12). Genetic counseling reveals that this translocation carrier state poses a risk for offspring with unbalanced chromosome complements. The couple is attempting in vitro fertilization (IVF) with preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) to select embryos with a normal chromosome number. During PGT analysis of blastocysts, one embryo is found to have a karyotype of 45,XX,-21. Another embryo has a karyotype of 47,XX,+21,+22. A third embryo has a karyotype of 46,XX,der(21;22)(q10;q12). A fourth embryo has a karyotype of 46,XX. Which of the following is the most likely outcome for the embryo with the karyotype 45,XX,-21 if it were to be implanted and develop?

(A) Normal development leading to a viable birth, potentially with some phenotypic abnormalities related to the missing chromosome 21 material.
(B) Early embryonic lethality due to haploinsufficiency of essential genes located on chromosome 21.
(C) Development into a viable fetus with Turner syndrome (45,X), characterized by short stature and ovarian dysgenesis.
(D) Development into a viable fetus with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), exhibiting characteristic facial features and intellectual disability.
(E) Development into a viable fetus with a phenotype similar to DiGeorge syndrome (22q11.2 deletion syndrome), due to the loss of chromosome 22 material.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- The patient has a balanced translocation t(21;22).
- This translocation can lead to unbalanced gametes during meiosis.
- The embryo with karyotype 45,XX,-21 has lost the entire chromosome 21.
- The question asks for the likely outcome of an embryo with complete autosomal monosomy (loss of chromosome 21).
- The key concept is the viability of monosomies for autosomes versus sex chromosomes.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Autosomal Monosomy:** The loss of a complete autosome (e.g., chromosome 21) results in a karyotype like 45,XX,-21 or 45,XY,-21. This means that for every gene on that autosome, the individual has only one copy instead of the usual two.
- **Haploinsufficiency:** Many genes are essential for early development and viability. Having only one functional copy (haploinsufficiency) of such a gene is insufficient for normal development. These genes are often referred to as "essential genes."
- **Essential Genes on Autosomes:** Autosomes carry a large number of essential genes required for fundamental cellular processes, organogenesis, and overall embryonic development. The loss of an entire autosome means the loss of hundreds or thousands of genes, many of which are essential.
- **Lethality:** Due to the widespread haploinsufficiency of essential genes, complete autosomal monosomy is invariably lethal early in embryonic development, typically before implantation or very early post-implantation. The embryo fails to develop properly and is spontaneously aborted.
- **Sex Chromosome Monosomy:** In contrast, monosomy for a sex chromosome (e.g., 45,X, Turner syndrome) is compatible with life. This is because the X chromosome carries many genes essential for development, but females have two X chromosomes, and one can be inactivated (forming a Barr body) to achieve dosage compensation. Furthermore, the Y chromosome carries relatively few essential genes (primarily related to male sex determination). Therefore, having only one X chromosome is survivable, although it leads to specific clinical features.
- **Conclusion:** The embryo with karyotype 45,XX,-21 lacks an entire chromosome 21. Chromosome 21 contains numerous essential genes. The loss of these genes leads to widespread haploinsufficiency, resulting in early embryonic lethality.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** While monosomy for chromosome 21 results in the loss of chromosome 21 material, complete autosomal monosomy is lethal. This option suggests viability, which is incorrect for autosomal monosomy. The phenotype associated with partial trisomy 21 (e.g., due to an unbalanced translocation) is different from complete monosomy.
- (C): **Incorrect.** This describes Turner syndrome (45,X), which is a sex chromosome monosomy. While it is survivable, the embryo in the vignette has monosomy for an autosome (chromosome 21), not a sex chromosome. The clinical features described (short stature, ovarian dysgenesis) are specific to Turner syndrome, not autosomal monosomy.
- (D): **Incorrect.** This describes Down syndrome (Trisomy 21), which is the presence of an extra chromosome 21 (47,XX,+21 or 47,XY,+21). The embryo in the vignette lacks chromosome 21 (monosomy), not has an extra copy. The clinical features described are characteristic of trisomy 21, not monosomy 21.
- (E): **Incorrect.** This describes a phenotype related to the loss of chromosome 22 material, which might occur with a different unbalanced gamete (e.g., 45,XX,-22 or 46,XX,-22). While the parent has a translocation involving chromosome 22, the specific embryo in question (45,XX,-21) lacks chromosome 21, not chromosome 22. DiGeorge syndrome is typically caused by a microdeletion on 22q11.2, not a complete loss of chromosome 22.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- Complete autosomal monosomy is incompatible with life due to haploinsufficiency of essential genes. Sex chromosome monosomy (e.g., 45,X) is generally survivable.
- **Differentials:** Compare autosomal monosomy (lethal) vs. autosomal trisomy (e.g., Down syndrome, Edwards syndrome, Patau syndrome - survivable but with severe phenotypes) vs. sex chromosome monosomy (e.g., Turner syndrome - survivable) vs. sex chromosome trisomy (e.g., Klinefelter syndrome, Trisomy X - survivable). Understand the concept of essential genes and haploinsufficiency.

<a id='question-049'></a>

### Question 049: Meiotic Nondisjunction
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Meiotic nondisjunction is the failure of homologous chromosomes or sister chromatids to separate properly during meiosis I or meiosis II, respectively, leading to aneuploidy (abnormal chromosome number) in the resulting gametes and potentially causing genetic disorders like Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). (减数分裂的同源染色体或姐妹染色单体分离不正常，导致配子中染色体数目异常，可能引起唐氏综合征等遗传疾病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old primigravida presents for routine prenatal screening at 16 weeks gestation. Her family history is unremarkable. Amniocentesis is performed, and fetal karyotyping reveals 47,XY,+21. The mother undergoes further genetic counseling and testing. Her own karyotype shows 46,XX,normal. A detailed analysis of her meiotic divisions during oogenesis is conducted. Microscopic examination of metaphase I reveals a failure of homologous chromosomes 21 to separate properly during anaphase I, resulting in one daughter cell receiving both copies of chromosome 21 and the other receiving none. During meiosis II, the chromosome 21-containing cell proceeds normally, while the cell lacking chromosome 21 degenerates. This error in chromosome segregation occurred during which specific stage of meiosis?

(A) Anaphase II
(B) Metaphase II
(C) Anaphase I
(D) Metaphase I
(E) Prophase II

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old pregnant woman. Age is a known risk factor for meiotic nondisjunction.
- **Fetal Karyotype:** 47,XY,+21 indicates Trisomy 21 (Down syndrome) in a male fetus. This confirms aneuploidy.
- **Maternal Karyotype:** 46,XX,normal indicates the mother is chromosomally normal, meaning the nondisjunction event occurred during gametogenesis (oogenesis in this case), not inherited from the mother.
- **Meiotic Analysis:** The description explicitly states "failure of homologous chromosomes 21 to separate properly during anaphase I". This directly identifies the stage and type of nondisjunction.
- **Consequence:** One oocyte receives both chromosome 21s, and the other receives none. The cell lacking chromosome 21 degenerates (aneuploidy is lethal). The cell with both chromosome 21s is fertilized by a normal sperm (23,X), resulting in the trisomic zygote (47,XX,+21). The question asks about the mother's meiotic division, which produced the gamete with the extra chromosome 21.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Meiosis Overview:** Meiosis is a two-stage cell division process that produces gametes (sperm and eggs) with half the number of chromosomes as the parent cell.
    - **Meiosis I:** Homologous chromosomes pair up (synapsis), exchange genetic material (crossing over), and then separate. This reduces the chromosome number from diploid (2n) to haploid (n).
    - **Meiosis II:** Sister chromatids separate, similar to mitosis.
- **Nondisjunction:** Failure of chromosomes or chromatids to separate correctly.
    - **Nondisjunction I:** Failure of homologous chromosomes to separate during Anaphase I. Results in two gametes with n+1 chromosomes and two gametes with n-1 chromosomes.
    - **Nondisjunction II:** Failure of sister chromatids to separate during Anaphase II. Results in one gamete with n+1 chromosomes, one gamete with n-1 chromosomes, and two normal gametes (n).
- **Trisomy 21:** The presence of three copies of chromosome 21. In this case, it resulted from nondisjunction I in the mother's oogenesis. Homologous chromosomes 21 failed to separate in Anaphase I. One secondary oocyte received both copies of chromosome 21, and the other received none. The cell lacking chromosome 21 degenerated. The cell with two copies of chromosome 21 was fertilized by a normal sperm (23,X), leading to a zygote with 47,XX,+21.
- **The question specifically asks about the stage where the error occurred in the mother's meiosis.** The vignette clearly states this was during Anaphase I, when homologous chromosomes failed to separate.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Anaphase II: Nondisjunction in Anaphase II involves the failure of sister chromatids to separate. This would lead to one gamete with n+1, one with n-1, and two normal gametes. While it can cause aneuploidy, the vignette specifically describes the failure of *homologous* chromosomes to separate, which occurs in Meiosis I.
- (B) Metaphase II: Metaphase II is the stage where chromosomes (each consisting of two sister chromatids) align at the metaphase plate *before* separation in Anaphase II. Nondisjunction is a failure of *separation*, which occurs during anaphase, not metaphase.
- (D) Metaphase I: Metaphase I is the stage where homologous chromosome pairs align at the metaphase plate *before* separation in Anaphase I. Nondisjunction is a failure of *separation*, which occurs during anaphase, not metaphase.
- (E) Prophase II: Prophase II occurs after Meiosis I and before Metaphase II. It involves chromosome condensation and spindle formation. Nondisjunction is a failure of chromosome segregation during anaphase, not a stage like prophase.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- **High-Yield Takeaway:** Meiotic nondisjunction is the primary cause of aneuploidy (e.g., Trisomy 21, Trisomy 18, Trisomy 13, Monosomy X). Nondisjunction can occur in Meiosis I (affecting homologous chromosomes) or Meiosis II (affecting sister chromatids), leading to different gamete compositions. The stage of nondisjunction determines the resulting aneuploidy.
- **Differentials:** Understand the difference between nondisjunction I and nondisjunction II in terms of the chromosomes involved (homologous vs. sister chromatids) and the resulting gamete aneuploidy patterns. Also, differentiate aneuploidy from polyploidy (e.g., triploidy) and chromosomal structural rearrangements (e.g., translocations, deletions).

---

<a id='question-050'></a>

### Question 050: Nondisjunction in Meiosis I
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Nondisjunction during meiosis I leads to aneuploidy in gametes, specifically resulting in 50% of gametes having an extra chromosome (disomic) and 50% having a missing chromosome (nullisomic) for that homologous pair. This contrasts with meiosis II nondisjunction, which produces a mix of euploid and aneuploid gametes. (减数第一次分裂的互补染色单体分离失败会导致配子中出现染色体数目异常，具体而言，50%的配子会多一条染色体（二体），50%的配子会少一条染色体（零体）。这与减数第二次分裂的不分离不同，后者会产生混合的单倍体和染色体数目异常的配子。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents to a genetics clinic after experiencing three consecutive spontaneous miscarriages in the first trimester. She and her husband underwent standard infertility workup, including karyotyping. The female's karyotype reveals a balanced reciprocal translocation between chromosomes 14 and 21, denoted as t(14;21)(q21;q10). Her husband's karyotype is normal (46,XY). The couple is concerned about the risk of having a child with Down syndrome (Trisomy 21). Genetic counseling indicates that the mother is a carrier of a Robertsonian translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21. The mother's phenotype is normal because she has two copies of the long arms of chromosomes 14 and 21, and the short arms are fused into a small marker chromosome. However, during oogenesis, the homologous chromosomes 14 and 21, along with the translocated chromosome 14/21, can pair in a trivalent structure. This abnormal pairing can lead to the formation of gametes with an unbalanced number of chromosomes 14 and 21. Specifically, what proportion of the mother's oocytes are expected to contain trisomy 21?

(A) 0%
(B) 12.5%
(C) 25%
(D) 50%
(E) 75%

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old female with recurrent miscarriages, suggesting potential chromosomal abnormalities in the offspring.
- **Karyotype:** Mother has a balanced reciprocal translocation t(14;21)(q21;q10). This means parts of chromosome 14 and chromosome 21 have swapped places. She is phenotypically normal because she still has the necessary genetic material, albeit rearranged.
- **Mechanism:** During meiosis I in oogenesis, the normal chromosomes 14 and 21, along with the translocated chromosome (carrying parts of both 14 and 21), form a trivalent structure.
- **Nondisjunction:** This trivalent structure is unstable and prone to errors during segregation in meiosis I. The most common segregation pattern is alternate segregation, where either chromosome 14 or chromosome 21 goes to one daughter cell, and the other chromosome goes to the other daughter cell, along with the translocated chromosome.
- **Gamete Types:** This alternate segregation leads to four possible types of gametes:
    1.  Normal (chromosome 14 and chromosome 21) - 25%
    2.  Trisomy 21 (two copies of chromosome 21, one normal, one translocated) - 25%
    3.  Trisomy 14 (two copies of chromosome 14, one normal, one translocated) - 25%
    4.  Nullisomy 14 and Nullisomy 21 (no chromosome 14, no chromosome 21) - 25%
- **Question:** The question asks for the proportion of oocytes expected to contain trisomy 21.
- **Calculation:** Based on the segregation patterns, 25% of the oocytes will have trisomy 21.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
The patient carries a balanced reciprocal translocation t(14;21). During meiosis I, the homologous chromosomes 14 and 21, along with the derivative chromosome (carrying segments from both 14 and 21), form a trivalent structure. This trivalent is prone to errors during anaphase I segregation. The primary mode of segregation is alternate segregation. This means that either the normal chromosome 14 and the derivative chromosome 14/21 go to one pole, while the normal chromosome 21 goes to the other pole, OR the normal chromosome 21 and the derivative chromosome 14/21 go to one pole, while the normal chromosome 14 goes to the other pole. This results in four types of gametes, each produced in roughly equal proportions (25% each):
1.  **Normal:** Contains one normal chromosome 14 and one normal chromosome 21.
2.  **Trisomy 21:** Contains two copies of the long arm of chromosome 21 (one normal chromosome 21 and the derivative chromosome 14/21). This leads to Down syndrome if fertilization occurs with a normal sperm.
3.  **Trisomy 14:** Contains two copies of the long arm of chromosome 14 (one normal chromosome 14 and the derivative chromosome 14/21). This is usually lethal.
4.  **Nullisomy 14/21:** Contains neither chromosome 14 nor chromosome 21. This is also usually lethal.
Therefore, 25% of the gametes will be trisomic for chromosome 21. This represents a significantly increased risk compared to the general population risk for Down syndrome (approximately 1%).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): 0% - Incorrect. While the mother is phenotypically normal, the translocation disrupts normal chromosome segregation during meiosis, leading to a significant proportion of unbalanced gametes, including trisomy 21.
- (B): 12.5% - Incorrect. This value might arise from misinterpreting the segregation patterns or confusing it with other types of translocations or nondisjunction events. The alternate segregation pattern in a reciprocal translocation involving these specific chromosomes yields four distinct gamete types, each in roughly 25% proportion.
- (D): 50% - Incorrect. This proportion is typically associated with nondisjunction events in meiosis II, where one cell division fails, leading to two aneuploid cells and two euploid cells, or with nondisjunction of the sex chromosomes in males. Nondisjunction in meiosis I involving homologous chromosomes typically results in 50% of gametes being aneuploid and 50% being euploid (if it's a simple nondisjunction of one pair), or, as in this case with a trivalent, leads to four different types of gametes in roughly equal proportions.
- (E): 75% - Incorrect. This value is not consistent with the known segregation patterns of a balanced reciprocal translocation involving chromosomes 14 and 21. The alternate segregation mechanism produces four distinct gamete types, each theoretically occurring with 25% frequency.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Balanced reciprocal translocations involving autosomes, particularly chromosomes 14 and 21, significantly increase the risk of producing unbalanced gametes, including trisomies, leading to conditions like Down syndrome. The segregation pattern in meiosis I involving a trivalent structure typically results in four types of gametes, each in approximately 25% proportion. This contrasts with Robertsonian translocations, which often lead to a higher proportion of trisomies (around 10-15%) due to adjacent-1 segregation. Key differentials include Robertsonian translocations, meiotic nondisjunction (Meiosis I vs. Meiosis II), and uniparental disomy.

---
